MC	<p>2cf2_ded5</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. MC	<p>6c27_20fa</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. MC	<p>845a_b75e</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. MC	<p>00fa_d63c</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. MC	<p>f226_dd40</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. MC	<p>0b38_ea77</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. MC	<p>c8ec_9a32</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. MC	<p>805d_78f8</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. MC	<p>eb0a_b8cd</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. MC	<p>46a4_862e</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- GGACAT --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; border: 1px solid #999; display:inline-table;"> <tr><td style="border: 1px solid #999; padding: 2px 6px; text-align: center; font-family: monospace; font-size: 12pt;">3&prime;&ndash;</td><td style="border: 1px solid #999;
